← G1 · Graph call stack MAX / MLIR / CUSTOM OPERATIONS · SOURCE DEEP DIVE

ops.custom() · G1 and B0 changes

Complete copied Q&A · local objects, block contents, operation attributes and kernel analysis

QnA 1 · At each step, what changes in G1’s members and its block B0?

G1 continues to point to the same B0. The main transformation is that B0 gains one mo.custom operation and its symbolic result. Subsequent assignments update that operation’s attributes. Kernel verification can also populate the analysis objects owned through G1._kernel_library.

A field such as G1._current_block therefore keeps its existing reference while the block it references gains content. This follows the operation builder.

Use these illustrative names:

T0     = !mo.tensor<[32,32],f32,gpu:0>
OP1    = the new mo.custom operation
%o     = OP1's single symbolic result
TVout1 = the Python TensorValue wrapper around %o

Before ops.custom(), the relevant structure is:

G1
├─ _mlir_op ──────────────► mo.graph @fused_attention
├─ _current_block ──┐
└─ _graph_body ─────┴─────► B0

B0
├─ arguments
│  ├─ %q : T0
│  ├─ %k : T0
│  └─ %v : T0
└─ operations
   └─ ChainCreateOp
      └─ %ch0 : !mo.chain

The block aliases are established by the constructor. %q, %k, and %v are explanatory names for its three input arguments.

The initial argument-preparation steps work as follows. These statements are in custom().

Step in your tree Transformation of local objects Effect on G1 and B0
ops.custom(name=..., …) Python evaluates the arguments, then enters custom(). Function entry alone adds no operation.
Reuse q,k,v; construct output TensorType(...) Creates a Python output-type description: float32, shape [32,32], placement gpu:0. B0’s input arguments and their types remain unchanged. The new type description is supplied through out_types.
parameters: BD=8, BN=16; operands [...]; result type T Constructs the argument objects: {"BD":8,"BN":16}, [q,k,v], and [output_type]. These are initially caller arguments, rather than assignments to Graph members.
BN/BD are compile-time choices The two integers describe how the custom kernel should be specialized. G1._params remains {}. That dictionary tracks symbolic dimension names; BD/BN will become attributes on OP1.
graph=Graph.current → G1 Local variable graph receives the existing G1 object. Reads the active graph binding. It does not replace G1 or its block.
context=default_mlir_context() Local variable context receives the existing default context. No Graph member assignment occurs.
symbol_attr=StringAttr.get(...) Creates the MLIR string attribute containing "modular_ops::fused_attention_custom". The attribute is prepared locally; it is attached when OP1 is constructed.
Normalize device to DeviceRef(gpu,0) Local device becomes a device-placement description. G1’s module device-information dictionary and device chains remain unchanged.
Reject BufferValue/opaque inputs Checks the input-wrapper categories. These three TensorValues pass. Successful checking leaves B0 unchanged.
Normalize inputs to TensorValue wrappers Creates wrappers referring to the existing %q, %k, and %v. The underlying SSA values remain the same. No new block arguments or tensor-producing operations are added.

The wrapper behavior is explicit in the TensorValue constructor: wrapping an existing TensorValue retains its _mlir_value.

Next, the builder inserts OP1 into B0. This is where the block’s operation list changes.

Builder step What happens Effect on G1 / B0
[T.to_mlir()] Converts the output description into a native MO tensor type T0. Prepares the type of a future result. It does not create %o yet.
graph._add_op_get_op_with_results(...) Calls the builder with G1 as self, the result type, three operands, and symbol attribute. The builder works on G1’s existing block.
Unwrap lists recursively Converts Python wrappers into MLIR binding objects through their CAPI handles. The resulting operand handles still identify %q, %k, and %v; this does not clone the input IR.
mlir.InsertionPoint(self._body) Creates a local insertion-point object targeting B0. _body returns _current_block; neither _current_block nor _graph_body is reassigned.
with ip, _location() Activates the insertion point and location for operation construction. Establishes temporary construction state. The location is supplied to the new operation.
Capture diagnostics Attaches a temporary MLIR diagnostic handler. Its diagnostic list is local, not a new member of G1.
Call mo.custom(...) Constructs OP1, attaches its operands/symbol/result type, and inserts it at the selected point. B0 gains OP1. OP1 owns the new result %o : T0.
“Schematic body now…” Describes the insertion that just happened. No additional transformation happens merely from describing it.
last_operation(B0) → B0.back() Retrieves the operation just inserted: OP1. Reads B0’s operation list. It does not append another operation.
staged_op.opview Obtains a Python operation view for OP1. Another handle to the same native operation.
_verify_op(staged_op) Performs structural IR verification. On the successful path, verification leaves the operation and block unchanged.
_set_output_param_decls(..., G1._params) Examines symbolic parameters in OP1’s result types. [32,32] introduces none. G1._params stays {}, and no new output parameter declarations are added.
Wrap %o through Value.from_mlir Creates TVout1, whose _mlir_value refers to %o. %o already exists. Wrapping it creates a Python object, rather than another IR result.
Return ([TVout1], staged_op) Returns the result wrapper and OP1’s view to custom(). These become local variables results and custom_op; no G1.results member is assigned.

Sources: builder and insertion, structural verification, symbolic-output parameter handling.

Immediately after insertion, the ownership view becomes:

B0
├─ arguments: still THREE
│  ├─ %q : T0
│  ├─ %k : T0
│  └─ %v : T0
└─ operations: now TWO
   ├─ ChainCreateOp
   │  └─ result: %ch0
   └─ OP1 : mo.custom
      ├─ operands: [%q, %k, %v]
      ├─ result: %o : T0
      └─ symbol: "modular_ops::fused_attention_custom"

TVout1
└─ _mlir_value ──► %o
                   └─ defining operation ──► OP1

The following setters modify OP1’s attributes. They do not add further operations.

Step Before → after Where the change lives
Set custom_op.parameters Effective empty parameters → {BD = 8 : index, BN = 16 : index} OP1’s parameters attribute.
device.to_mlir() Python DeviceRef(gpu,0) → native #M.device_ref<"gpu",0> Initially a prepared attribute object.
Set custom_op.device Effective default placement → explicit gpu:0 OP1’s device attribute.
“Final call fields…” OP1 now carries the completed symbol, operands, result type, parameters and device. Describes the same OP1 after those assignments.

The custom-op definition has an effective default device of cpu:0 and default empty parameters. Defaults may be omitted from the stored attribute dictionary. This API supplies its explicit GPU device after insertion. See the custom-op attribute definition.

The integer conversion and setters are in custom.py.

The resulting operation is schematically:

OP1
├─ name       = mo.custom
├─ symbol     = "modular_ops::fused_attention_custom"
├─ operands   = [%q, %k, %v]
├─ results    = [%o : T0]
├─ parameters = {BD = 8 : index, BN = 16 : index}
└─ device     = gpu:0

BD/BN become compiler metadata on this operation. No separate mo.constant operations for 8 and 16 are inserted by these setters.

Kernel-library verification has a separate state change to consider.

custom() subsequently calls:

graph._kernel_library.verify_custom_op(custom_op)

The Python verification/cache path has two cases:

Case Transformation
Analysis-cache miss Uses the existing analysis object. Native verification can initialize its declaration module and registry. After successful verification, the analysis is stored in the class-level KernelLibrary._analysis_cache.
Analysis-cache hit Assigns the cached analysis object to G1._kernel_library._analysis, then verifies against it.

For a cold analysis object, the native ownership transformation is:

G1._kernel_library
└─ _analysis → Analysis A1
   ├─ libraryPathsSet
   │  └─ already contains P1's registered path
   ├─ kernelDecls
   │  └─ empty → standalone kernel-declaration module
   └─ registry
      └─ empty → MojoRegistry built from that module

kernelDecls and registry are private C++ fields, shown here as an ownership view. Native fields.

The lazy initialization loads package declarations into that standalone module. Those declarations are not appended to B0.

Verification checks the registered name, device-specific implementation, operand/result mapping and supplied compile-time parameters. The successful path leaves OP1 in B0 with its completed attributes. Native verification.

Finally, return results gives the caller [TVout1]. This is the return at custom.py:129.

At this point:

G1._current_block       → same B0
G1._graph_body          → same B0
G1._mlir_op             → same enclosing mo.graph

B0.arguments           → same three arguments
B0.operations          → ChainCreateOp + OP1

G1._weights            → {}
G1._params             → {}
G1._has_chain_input     → False
G1._always_ready_chain → same %ch0
G1.device_chains        → unchanged by this custom() call

caller results         → [TVout1]
TVout1._mlir_value      → %o, owned by OP1

This custom() route never accesses graph.device_chains[device] or calls _update_chain(). If an earlier lookup already created a gpu:0 entry, that entry remains as it was; this call does not advance it. See the lazy device-chain map.

The next statement, graph.output(*results), performs additional work: it updates the graph’s signature/output metadata and inserts mo.output(%o) into B0. Those changes happen after the steps above, inside Graph.output().

"""What a graph outputs: values, or a tree of them whose leaves are the
outputs in order. Sequences and mappings rather than :data:`max.tree.Tree`'s
lists and dicts, so a ``list[TensorValue]`` or a typed group is accepted.
Mapping keys only name the outputs, so any key type is."""
